_______________________________ test_data_stats ________________________________

metrics = {'T_median': 60.0, 'T_median_ok': False, 'T_median_ratio': 0.03333333333333333, 'contaminated_files': {}, ...}
submitted_data_stats = {'T_max': 60, 'T_mean': 60.0, 'T_median': 60.0, 'T_min': 60, ...}
reference_data_stats = {'chance_majority': {'position': 0.20088705149798397}, 'chance_uniform': {'position': 0.1111111111111111}, 'data_summary': {'T_max': 1800, 'T_mean': 1800.0, 'T_median': 1800.0, 'T_min': 1800, ...}, 'errors': [], ...}

    def test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Check the converted dataset matches the reference in size, variables and ranges.
    
        Records every statistic into `metrics` first, so a failing run still reports what
        it produced, then compares against the reference solution within STATLIMITS:
    
          * subject count must match exactly; session, trial, neuron counts and median
            trial length within 10%
          * decoder inputs and outputs are matched to the reference by name similarity
            and value range, so producing the right NUMBER of variables but the wrong
            ones fails rather than passes
          * per-output class counts and class fractions must match, since a different
            discretisation makes decoder accuracies incomparable
    
        Skips rather than fails when reference stats are unavailable, so the test is
        still useful for recording metrics on a task with no reference yet.
        """
    
        fields_check = [
            'nsessions',
            'ntrials_total',
            'T_median',
            'nsubjects',
            'nneurons_total',
        ]
    
        # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to
        # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream
        # reporting / grids.
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]
    
        # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE
        # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a
        # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)
        # need it for unsupervised tasks too.
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi))+1
    
        # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised
        # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the
        # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():
            metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]
    
        # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.
        metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))
        metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison")
            return
    
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]
            # Verdict recorded where the threshold is applied; the assert below reads it back.
            # The assert loop bails on its first failure, so computing these here is what makes
            # a later field's result knowable after an earlier one has failed.
            metrics[field+'_ok'] = bool(
                ratio_within_limits(metrics[field+'_ratio'], STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']))
    
        # Seed a per-variable verdict for every REFERENCE output, so a variable the agent never
        # produced -- and which therefore never appears in output_matches below -- counts as a
        # miss rather than shrinking the denominator of the fraction categories.
        reference_output_names = list(reference_data_stats['data_summary'].get('output_range') or [])
        for ref_name in reference_output_names:
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
    
        # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching
        input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(
            submitted_data_stats['input_range'],
            reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],
            weight_semantic=.95,
            weight_range=.05,
            weight_fraction=0,
            exact_range=False,
        )
    
        # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to
        # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every
        # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.
        # Named for what it is: the mean Hungarian MATCHING cost, which is dominated
        # by name similarity and is forced to 0 for exactly-matching names before any
        # range term is computed. It is not a mean of the input_range_error_<var>
        # fields -- that is mean_input_range_error, recorded below.
        mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0
        metrics['input_match_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost
        metrics['input_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in input_matches
        ]
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']
        ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]
            scale = abs(ref_hi - ref_lo)
            if scale < 1e-6 and abs(sub_hi - sub_lo) < 1e-6:
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = 0.0
            else:
                scale = max(scale, 1e-6)
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale))
        mean_input_range_error = np.mean([metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] for ref_name, _, _ in input_matches]) if len(input_matches) else 0.0
        metrics['mean_input_range_error'] = mean_input_range_error
        # Recorded ONLY when there is something to match. A task with no decoder inputs
        # (allen2p: dinput == 0, input_range == {}) has no input ranges to compare, so leaving
        # the key unset scores the category undefined and drops it from the mean -- rather than
        # awarding a free 1.0 for a check that never happened. Matches undefined_categories()
        # in lesion_analysis.py, which singles out this same case. The assert below is guarded
        # by the same condition, so it never reads a key that was not written.
        if len(input_matches):
            metrics['input_range_ok'] = bool(
                mean_input_range_error <= STATLIMITS['input_range_error'])
    
        # match outputs based on range, fraction, and semantic similarity of names
        output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
            submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
        )
        metrics['output_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in output_matches
        ]
    
        # As above: the mean matching cost, not a mean of output_fraction_error_<var>.
        mean_output_match_cost = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
        metrics['output_match_mean_cost'] = mean_output_match_cost
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']
        ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(abs(sub_hi - ref_hi))
            # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can
            # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.
            # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi + 1.
            # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the
            # `_reference_<var>` variant is the reference's n_classes.
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi) + 1)
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_reference_{ref_name}'] = int(round(ref_hi) + 1)
            # fraction error per output
            ref_fracs = sorted(reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'][ref_name].values())
            sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'][sub_name].values())
            if len(ref_fracs) == len(sub_fracs):
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = float(np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs))))
            else:
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = None
    
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] <= STATLIMITS['output_range_error'])
            # A None error means the class counts differ, so there is no comparable fraction
            # vector. That scores as a miss, but is NOT asserted on below -- the nclasses check
            # already fails that case, and asserting here too would change what the suite
            # rejects. Verdict and assert diverge only here, deliberately.
            frac_error = metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}']
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                frac_error is not None and frac_error <= STATLIMITS['output_fraction_error'])
    
        for field in fields_check:
>           assert metrics[field+'_ok'], f"{field} in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats[field]}) is not within {STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']*100}% of reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]})"
E           AssertionError: T_median in submitted data (60.0) is not within 10.0% of reference (1800.0)
E           assert False

The `position` variable records the animal's location in a 75×75 cm open field arena at each timepoint.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Position*?\n\ni. The 2D position is discretized into a 3×3 grid (9 classes). Each axis is divided into 3 equal bins spanning the 75 cm arena. The grid label is computed as `y_bin * 3 + x_bin`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_position(position, n_grid=N_GRID, arena_size=ARENA_SIZE):\n    edges = np.linspace(0, arena_size, n_grid + 1)[1:-1]\n    x_bin = np.clip(np.digitize(position[0], edges), 0, n_grid - 1)\n    y_bin = np.clip(np.digitize(position[1], edges), 0, n_grid - 1)\n    return (y_bin * n_grid + x_bin).astype(np.int8)\n```\n\niii. A 3×3 grid gives 9 position classes, providing a coarse but balanced spatial discretization. `np.clip` ensures positions at the arena boundaries are assigned to valid bins.\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Position and neural data are stored at the same frame rate in the `.mat` file, so they are aligned frame-for-frame. Both are split into trials using the same indices.\n\nii.\n```python\nneural_trials = split_into_trials(trace)\noutput_trials = split_into_trials(output)\n```\n\niii. Both arrays have the same number of timepoints and are sliced identically by `split_into_trials`, ensuring alignment.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Neurons with all-NaN values (not recorded in that session) are removed (see 2-c). Remainder frames that don't fill a complete 60-second trial are discarded.\n\nii.\n```python\nactive_mask = ~np.all(np.isnan(trace), axis=0)\ntrace = trace[:, active_mask].astype(np.float32).T\n...\nn_trials = data.shape[1] // trial_length  # remainder is implicitly dropped\n```\n\niii. NaN filtering ensures only actually recorded neurons are included. Discarding remainder frames is a minor data loss.\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is loading the `.mat` files via `h5py`, which is I/O bound.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The `.mat` files contain large neural trace arrays. Processing (NaN filtering, discretization, trial splitting) is fast by comparison.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\nN/A\n\n## 6-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\nN/A\n\n## 6-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\nN/A\n\n## 6-e. How is memory usage optimized?\n\ni. N/A\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. N/A","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Hz\nTIME_BIN_SIZE = 1000.0 / SAMPLING_RATE  # ~33.33 ms\nTRIAL_DURATION_SEC = 60  # 1 minute per trial\nTRIAL_LENGTH = SAMPLING_RATE * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC  # 1800 timepoints\n\n\ndef discretize_position(position, n_grid=N_GRID, arena_size=ARENA_SIZE):\n    \"\"\"Discretize (2, n_timepoints) position into grid class labels 0..n_grid^2-1.\"\"\"\n    edges = np.linspace(0, arena_size, n_grid + 1)[1:-1]\n    x_bin = np.clip(np.digitize(position[0], edges), 0, n_grid - 1)\n    y_bin = np.clip(np.digitize(position[1], edges), 0, n_grid - 1)\n    return (y_bin * n_grid + x_bin).astype(np.int8)\n\n\ndef encode_blocked(blk_indices, n_positions=N_BLOCKED_POSITIONS):\n    \"\"\"Convert blocked indices to one-hot encoding.\n\n    Args:\n        blk_indices: array of blocked position indices, or [-1] if none blocked\n        n_positions: total number of possible blocked positions\n\n    Returns:\n        (n_positions,) float32 array with 1s at blocked indices, 0s elsewhere\n    \"\"\"\n    blocked = np.zeros(n_positions, dtype=np.float32)\n    if not (len(blk_indices) == 1 and blk_indices[0] == -1):\n        blocked[blk_indices.astype(int)] = 1\n    return blocked\n\n\ndef split_into_trials(data, trial_length=TRIAL_LENGTH):\n    \"\"\"Split a (d, n_timepoints) or (n_timepoints,) array into 1-minute trials.\n\n    The last chunk is dropped if shorter than trial_length.\n\n    Returns:\n        list of arrays, each of shape (d, trial_length) or (trial_length,)\n    \"\"\"\n    if data.ndim == 1:\n        n_trials = len(data) // trial_length\n        return [data[i * trial_length:(i + 1) * trial_length] for i in range(n_trials)]\n    else:\n        n_trials = data.shape[1] // trial_length\n        return [data[:, i * trial_length:(i + 1) * trial_length] for i in range(n_trials)]\n\n\ndef process_mat_file(filepath):\n    \"\"\"Process a single .mat file.\n\n    Each recording session in the file becomes a separate session.\n    Each session is split into 1-minute trials.\n    Only neurons actually recorded are kept.\n\n    Returns:\n        sessions: list of dicts with keys 'neural', 'input', 'output', 'n_neurons'\n    \"\"\"\n    f = h5py.File(filepath, 'r')\n\n    trace_refs = f['trace']\n    pos_refs = f['position']\n    blk_refs = f['blocked'][:][0]\n    n_recording_sessions = trace_refs.shape[0]\n\n    sessions = []\n\n    for i in range(n_recording_sessions):\n        # trace: (timepoints, neurons) -> keep only recorded neurons -> (n_active, n_timepoints)\n        trace = f[trace_refs[i][0]][:]  # (timepoints, neurons)\n        active_mask = ~np.all(np.isnan(trace), axis=0)\n        trace = trace[:, active_mask].astype(np.float32).T  # (n_active, n_timepoints)\n\n        # position -> discretized class labels (1, n_timepoints)\n        position = f[pos_refs[i][0]][:].T  # (2, n_timepoints)\n        output = discretize_position(position)[np.newaxis, :]  # (1, n_timepoints)\n\n        # blocked -> one-hot (n_blocked_positions,)\n        blk_indices = f[blk_refs[i]][:].flatten()\n        blocked = encode_blocked(blk_indices)\n\n        # split into 1-minute trials\n        neural_trials = split_into_trials(trace)\n        output_trials = split_into_trials(output)\n        input_trials = [blocked] * len(neural_trials)\n\n        n_active = int(active_mask.sum())\n interchange format.\n\nDecisions\n---------\n* The repository's joblib files are used rather than reprocessing calcium video.\n  They contain the paper's aligned 30-Hz binary rising-transient traces and DLC\n  position, produced with the processing described in the paper.\n* A recording day is a session.  Neurons tracked across animals' days occupy a\n  union array; an all-NaN row means that cell was not observed that day.  Such\n  rows are removed session-wise.  This gives the reported 69,744 rate maps.\n* Complete, non-overlapping 60-s windows are trials.  A short trailing fragment\n  is omitted so all trials have identical duration.\n* Thirty synchronized frames are aggregated per 1-s decoder bin.  Binary neural\n  events are summed (event counts), while x/y is averaged.  This preserves event\n  mass and avoids an impractically large 30-Hz decoder dataset.\n* The 75-cm square is divided at 25 and 50 cm, exactly matching the experiment's\n  3x3 construction.  Class is row*3+column, with x selecting column and y row.\n* Geometry is the supplied `blocked` list, encoded as nine binary indicators.\n  The square's sentinel -1 means no blocked cells.\n\"\"\"\nfrom pathlib import Path\nimport gc\nimport pickle\nimport joblib\nimport numpy as np\n\nDATA_DIR = Path('/app/data')\nOUT = Path('/app/converted_data.pkl')\nSUBJECTS = ['QLAK-CA1-08', 'QLAK-CA1-30', 'QLAK-CA1-50',\n            'QLAK-CA1-51', 'QLAK-CA1-56', 'QLAK-CA1-74',\n            'QLAK-CA1-75']\nFS = 30\nBIN_FRAMES = 30\nTRIAL_SECONDS = 60\nBINS_PER_TRIAL = TRIAL_SECONDS\nTRIAL_FRAMES = FS * TRIAL_SECONDS\n\n\ndef scalar_env(v):\n    \"\"\"Normalize the (1,) numpy string used by the converted source.\"\"\"\n    a = np.asarray(v).reshape(-1)\n    return str(a[0])\n\n\ndef main():\n    neural, inputs, outputs = [], [], []\n    subject_idx, brain_region_idx, session_info = [], [], []\n    total_valid = 0\n\n    for subj_i, subject in enumerate(SUBJECTS):\n        print(f'Loading {subject}', flush=True)\n        source = joblib.load(DATA_DIR / subject)[subject]\n        traces = source['trace']       # day x union-neuron x frame\n        positions = source['position'] # day x (x,y) x frame\n        envs = source['envs']\n        blocked = source['blocked']\n\n        for day in range(traces.shape[0]):\n            tr = np.asarray(traces[day])\n            pos = np.asarray(positions[day])\n            # Source conversion puts features first.      used = n_trials * TRIAL_FRAMES\n\n            # (neuron, trial, second, frame-within-second), then trial first.\n            counts = tr[:, :used].reshape(\n                n_neurons, n_trials, BINS_PER_TRIAL, BIN_FRAMES\n            ).sum(axis=3, dtype=np.float32).transpose(1, 0, 2)\n            mean_pos = pos[:, :used].reshape(\n                2, n_trials, BINS_PER_TRIAL, BIN_FRAMES\n            ).mean(axis=3)\n            xbin = np.clip(np.floor(mean_pos[0] / 25.0), 0, 2).astype(np.int64)\n            ybin = np.clip(np.floor(mean_pos[1] / 25.0), 0, 2).astype(np.int64)\n            spatial_class = ybin * 3 + xbin\n\n            geometry = np.zeros(9, dtype=np.float32)\n            block_values = np.asarray(blocked[day], dtype=float).reshape(-1)\n            for b in block_values:\n                if np.isfinite(b) and 0 <= int(b) < 9:\n                    geometry[int(b)] = 1.0\n\n            neural.append([counts[t] for t in range(n_trials)])\n            inputs.append([geometry.copy() for _ in range(n_trials)])\n            outputs.append([spatial_class[t][None, :] for t in range(n_trials)])\n            subject_idx.append(subj_i)\n            brain_region_idx.append(np.zeros(n_neurons, dtype=np.int64))\n            env = scalar_env(envs[day])\n            session_info.append({\n                'subject': subject,\n                'source_session_index': day,\n                'environment': env,\n                'blocked_grid_indices': [int(b) for b in block_values if b >= 0],\n                'source_frames': int(n_frames),\n                'used_frames': int(used),\n                'n_complete_one_minute_trials': int(n_trials),\n                'n_neurons_present': int(n_neurons),\n            })\n            total_valid               'used_frames': int(used),\n                'n_complete_one_minute_trials': int(n_trials),\n                'n_neurons_present': int(n_neurons),\n            })\n            total_valid += n_neurons\n\n        del source, traces, positions\n        gc.collect()\n\n    if len(neural) != 207 or total_valid != 69744:\n        raise RuntimeError(f'unexpected source totals: {len(neural)} behav_dict\ndrwxr-xr-x 2 root root      4096 Apr  8  2025 precomputed_results\n---\nWe recorded from large populations in hippocampal subregion CA1 in a condition-rich geometric deformation paradigm (5,413 unique neurons across 207 sessions in 10 geometries, forming 69,744 rate maps).\n\nWe partitioned an open square (75 × 75 cm) into a 3 × 3 grid space to allow systematic manipulations of environmental geometry. We recorded large neural populations in CA1 with miniscope calcium imaging while mice freely explored a sequence of 10 geometrically distinct environments (Figures 1A, 1B, and S1). Importantly, we selected these distinct geometries to induce strong remapping within the same environment across the entire CA1 population. Following habituation to the square arena, animals were exposed to a random sequence of geometries across days that started and ended with the square. The same sequence was repeated up to three times within animals but was randomized across mice (Figures 1C and 1D).\n\nMethods\n\nIn vivo calcium videos were recorded with a UCLA miniscope (v3; miniscope.org) containing a monochrome CMOS imaging sensor (MT9V032C12STM, ON Semiconductor) connected to a custom data acquisition (DAQ) box (miniscope.org) with a lightweight, flexible coaxial cable. The DAQ was connected to a PC with a USB 3.0 SuperSpeed cable and controlled with Miniscope custom acquisition software (miniscope.org\n). The outgoing excitation LED was set to between 2-8% (∼0.05-0.2 mW), depending on the mouse to maximize signal quality with the minimum possible excitation light to mitigate the risk of photobleaching. Gain was adjusted to match the dynamic range of the recorded video to the fluctuations of the calcium signal for each recording to avoid saturation. Behavioral video data were recorded by a webcam mounted above the environment. The DAQ simultaneously acquired behavioral and cellular imaging streams at 30 Hz as uncompressed AVI files and all recorded frames were timestamped for post-hoc alignment.\nAll recording environments were constructed of white Lego base plates (Lego, Inc) and white painted plywood walls. The full square environment was 75 cm x 75 cm. The external walls were 60 cm tall, while inserted partition walls were 25 cm tall. A blue Lego baseplate was affixed at the top-center of the Eastern wall to serve as an orienting visual feature. During recording, the environment was dimly lit. All sessions were 40 min, and one session was recorded per day to avoid photobleaching. The order of the environmental configurations was randomized for each mouse, but repeated across sequences such that repeated configurations were always equidistant in time.\n\nData preprocessing\n\nCalcium imaging data were preprocessed prior to analyses via a pipeline of open-source MATLAB (MathWorks; version R2015a) functions to correct for motion artifacts,47 segment cells and extract transients.45,48 To extract the rising phase of transients from each filtered calcium trace, we proceeded as follows. First, we computed the derivative of the calcium signal, smoothed with a gaussian kernel with a standard deviation of 5 frames. Next, because calcium transients around the baseline can only be positive, we estimated the variance in the derivative of the smoothed calcium signal on the basis of noise via a half-normal distribution such that:\n\n<math><mrow is=\"true\"><mi is=\"true\">N</mi><mi is=\"true\">O</mi><mi is=\"true\">I</mi><mi is=\"true\">S</mi><mi is=\"true\">E</mi><mo linebreak=\"badbreak\" is=\"true\">=</mo><mfrac is=\"true\"><mrow is=\"true\"><mi is=\"true\">s</mi><mi is=\"true\">t</mi><mi is=\"true\">d</mi><mrow is=\"true\"><mo is=\"true\">(</mo><mrow is=\"true\"><mo is=\"true\">Δ</mo><mi is=\"true\">t</mi><mrow is=\"true\"><mo is=\"true\">(</mo><mrow is=\"true\"><mi is=\"true\">t</mi><mo linebreak=\"badbreak\" is=\"true\">&lt;</mo><mn is=\"true\">0</mn></mrow><mo is=\"true\">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo is=\"true\">)</mo></mrow></mrow><msqrt is=\"true\"><mrow is=\"true\"><mn is=\"true\">1</mn><mo linebreak=\"badbreak\" is=\"true\">−</mo><mfrac is=\"true\"><mn is=\"true\">2</mn><mi is=\"true\">π</mi></mfrac></mrow></msqrt></mfrac></mrow></math>\n\nWhere is the smoothed time-derivative of the median-subtracted calcium trace . We then z-scored on the basis of this noise distribution. The final binarized rising-phase vector was then set to 1 whenever this z-scored vector exceeded 2.5, and 0 otherwise. This binary vector was treated as the firing rate in all further analyses. The motion-corrected calcium imaging data were manually inspected to ensure that motion correction was effective and did not introduce additional artifacts. Following this preprocessing pipeline, the spatial footprints of all cells were manually verified to remove lens artifacts. Position data were generated from tracking the head with DeepLabCut pose-estimation software.46 Cells were tracked across sessions on the basis of prominent brain surface landmarks, their spatial footprints, and/or centroids.\n\nData analysis\n\nAll analyses were conducted using the binary vector of the rising phases of transients, treating this vector as if it were the firing rate of the cell (henceforth firing rate). Similar results are observed when the likelihood of spiking was inferred via a second-order autoregressive deconvolution instead of transient rising-phase extraction. All statistical comparisons were performed with Scipy and Statsmodels libraries in Python 3.8.\n\nPlace cell identification\n\nTo detect place cells, we generated rate maps for each session half (first and second 20 min) and compared the resulting Pearson correlation to the maps generated from 1000 circular shuffles or the corresponding position data. Place cells were identified as those whose split-half rate map correlation exceeded the 99th percentile of the corresponding shuffled distribution for each cell.\n\nBayesian decoding of animal position\n\nTo decode animals’ position from calcium traces within each session, we performed a 5-fold split of spatially binned position and trace data and transformed the binned positions to a one-hot vector.  Decoding error was then estimated as the Euclidean distance between the predicted and actual spatial bin position of the animal on withheld />\n\n___________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________\n# Setup and running code\n\nTo clone the repository you can 1) open terminal or command prompt, 2) navigate to the desired working directory, and 3) run the following to clone the repository:\n```\n$ git clone https://github.com/jquinnlee/georepca1.git\n```\n\nTo run code with all dependencies installed you can **create virtual environment from the environment.yml file** in the command line: \n```\n$ conda env create --name georepca1 --file environment.yml\n```\n\nFinally, in Python add the src folder (containing all custom functions and main.py) to your path:\n```\n$ import sys\n$ sys.path.append(\"your_working_directory_name/georepca1/src\")\n```\n\nDataset can be freely downloaded from **Zenodo** (https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.13993254) and **manually added to the \"georepca1/data\" folder**.\n\nTo run all main analyses and generate figures, you can **either run main.py** after defining your local path to the georepca1 folder, or follow through **Jupyter notebooks** located in the \"georepca1/demos\" folder.\n*Note that some results build across notebooks, and thus each should be run to completion before moving onto the next.*\n\n___________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________\n# Data organization\n\nThe dataset (Python joblib files or MATLAB .mat files) are given names of animal IDs from the original study that can be downloaded from Zenodo and contain the following fields in each file:\n\n**SFPs**: spatial footprints (also known as ROI) for every registered cell, centered over each cell. Shape - Dimx, dimy, number of SFPs (ROIs), number of days. If cell is not registered it will be nan along dimx and dimy for a given day.\n\n**blocked**: location of blocked (occluded) partitions in 3x3 design of environment. Location of partitions are shown in paper, but are organized in the following way – [[0, 1, 2], [3, 4, 5], [6, 7, 8]]. If no partitions are blocked, value is -1.\n\n**centroids**: centroid of spatial footprint. Shape – number of cells, x-y location, number of days.\n\n**envs**: environment shape identified with string name \n\n**maps**: three types of maps generated from the dataset. “sampling” is the occupancy of animal in each spatial bin, shape – xbins, ybins, number of days. “smoothed” is the event rate map smoothed with 2.5 cm gaussian kernel, shape – xbins, ybins, number of cells, number of days. “unsmoothed” is the same event rate map data without smoothing.\n\n**position**: x-y position data for all days. List shape number of days, with shape on each day indicating x-y position in first dimension, and number of temporal bins / frames in second dimension.\n\n**trace**: rise-extracted calcium traces, where “1” indicates a significant event. See paper for details on processing pipeline. If cell is not registered on given day, will appear as nan the same shape.\n\n___________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________\n# Cite\n**Please cite our original paper by Lee et al. temporally bin trace and behavioral data\n    pooling = AvgPool1d(kernel_size=temporal_bin_size, stride=temporal_bin_size)\n    behav, traces = pooling(torch.tensor(behav.T)).numpy().astype(int).T, \\\n        pooling(torch.tensor(gaussian_filter1d(traces, sigma=temporal_bin_size, axis=0).T)).numpy().T\n    # temporally bin behav data\n    pooling = AvgPool1d(kernel_size=temporal_bin_size, stride=temporal_bin_size)\n    behav, traces = pooling(torch.tensor(behav.T)).numpy().astype(int).T,\\\n                    pooling(torch.tensor(gaussian_filter1d(traces, sigma=temporal_bin_size, axis=0).T)).numpy().T\n    # calculate the squared distance between actual and predicted behavior\n    err_squared = np.zeros(n_samples)\n    for i in range(n_samples):\n        err_squared[i] = (bin_down * np.linalg.norm(predictions_transform[i] - behav[i])) ** 2\n    return behav, predictions_transform, err_squared, predictions_map\n\n\ndef decode_position_within(behav, traces, maps, n_bins=15, fps=30, v_filt_size=5,\n                           v_thresh=5, cell_threshold=5, buffer=1e-15, n_fold=5):\n    \"\"\"\n    Decode animal temporal and spatially binned position with k-fold cross validation and naive Bayesian method.\n    :param behav: x-y position of animal shape n frames (row) by n features (x and y position in columns)\n    :param traces: calcium trace data shape n frames (row) by n cells (column)\n    :param maps: pre-computed event rate maps from calcium and position data\n    :param n_bins: number of spatial bins for x and y position\n    :param fps: frames per second of original data\n    :param v_filt_size: size of temporal window of filter used for velocity estimates\n    :param v_thresh: threshold for velocity to be included in fitting and predictions\n    :param cell_threshold: threshold for activity sparsity of cells to be included in fitting and predictions\n    :param buffer: buffer size for rounding of position\n    :param n_fold: number of cross validation folds\n    :return: distances - Euclidean error in position decoding at each binned time point, np.sqrt(mse) - spatial map of\n    average Euclidean error between actual and decoded position, imse - inverse mse, coherence_maps - coherence map of\n    decoding errors\n    \"\"\"\n    # determine maxima of behavioral features across all days (needed for across-day training and decoding)\n    behav_max = (behav.max(axis=0).max(axis=1) + buffer).astype(int)\n    # grab the number of days to iterate across, and initialize some variables to return and use as function params\n    n_days = behav.shape[2]\n    n_cells = traces.shape[1]\n    # create template maps for all days to find nearest x-y bins for dist_maps after predictions are made (cleaning)\n    temp_maps = np.nansum(maps, axis=2).astype(bool)\n    # initialize distance maps to show distance between actual and decoded position in each bin\n    mse, imse, coherence_maps = np.zeros_like(temp_maps).astype(float), np.zeros_like(temp_maps).astype(float),\\\n        np.zeros_like(temp_maps).astype(float)\n    for d in tqdm(range(n_days), desc=f'Performing {n_fold}-fold cross-validated position decoding'):\n        vel_idx[d, 1:] = gaussian_filter1d(np.linalg.norm((behav[1:, :, d] - behav[:-1, :, d]) * fps, axis=1),\n                                           axis=0, sigma=v_filt_size) > (v_thresh / bin_down)\n        cell_idx[d] = np.sum(traces[:, :, d][vel_idx[d]], axis=0) > cell_threshold\n        target_behav = behav[:, :, d][vel_idx[d]]\n        target_traces = traces[:, cell_idx[d], d][vel_idx[d]]\n        kf = KFold(n_splits=n_fold)\n        kf.get_n_splits(target_traces)\n        for fold, (train_index, test_index) in enumerate(kf.split(target_traces)):\n            # reverse-transform predictions\n    n_samples, n_features = behav.shape[0], behav.shape[1]\n    predictions_transform = np.zeros([n_samples, n_features])\n    empty_map = np.zeros(feature_max)\n    predictions_map = np.zeros([feature_max[0], feature_max[1], feature_max[0], feature_max[1]])\n    for i in range(n_samples):\n        trans = empty_map.flatten()\n        trans[predictions[i]] += 1\n        empty_map[np.where(trans.reshape(empty_map.shape))] += 1\n        predictions_map[behav[i][0], behav[i][1]] += empty_map/n_samples\n        predictions_transform[i] = np.array(np.where(empty_map)).squeeze()\n        empty_map *= 0\n    # calculate the squared distance between actual and predicted behavior\n    err_squared = np.zeros(n_samples)\n    for i in range(n_samples):\n        err_squared[i] = (bin_down * np.linalg.norm(predictions_transform[i] - behav[i])) ** 2\n    return behav, predictions_transform, err_squared, predictions_map\n\n\ndef decode_position_within(behav, traces, maps, n_bins=15, fps=30, v_filt_size=5,\n                           v_thresh=5, cell_threshold=5, buffer=1e-15, n_fold=5):\n    \"\"\"\n    Decode animal temporal and spatially binned position with k-fold cross validation and naive Bayesian method.\n    :param behav: x-y position of animal shape n frames (row) by n features (x and y position in columns)\n    :param traces: calcium trace data shape n frames (row) by n cells (column)\n    :param maps: pre-computed event rate maps from calcium and position data\n    :param n_bins: number of spatial bins for x and y position\n    :param fps: frames per second of original data\n    :param v_filt_size: size of temporal window of filter used for velocity estimates\n    :param v_thresh: threshold for velocity to be included in fitting and predictions\n    :param cell_threshold: threshold for activity sparsity of cells to be included in fitting and predictions\n    :param buffer: buffer size for rounding of position\n    :param n_fold: number of cross validation folds\n    :return: distances - Euclidean error in position decoding at each binned time point, np.sqrt(mse) - spatial map of\n    average Euclidean error between actual and decoded position, imse - inverse mse, coherence_maps - coherence map of\n    decoding errors\n    \"\"\"\n    # make a deepcopy of behav and trace data as to not change original inputs\n    behav = deepcopy(behav)\n    traces = deepcopy(traces)\n    # bin down behavioural data\n    behav_max = behav.max(axis=0).max(axis=1)\n    bin_down = (behav_max.max() + buffer) / n_bins\n    behav /= bin_down\n    # determine maxima of behavioral features across all days (needed for across-day training and decoding)\n    behav_max = (behav.max(axis=0).max(axis=1) + buffer).astype(int)\n    # grab the number of days to iterate across, and initialize some variables to return and use as function params\n    n_days = behav.shape[2]\n    n_cells = traces.shape[1]\n    # distances will contain the distance errors for model predictions\n    distances = np.zeros([n_days, n_fold])\n    # initialize velocity and cell idx for selecting data based on criteria\n    cell_idx = np.zeros([n_days, n_cells]).astype(bool)\n    vel_idx = np.zeros([n_days, behav.shape[0]]).astype(bool)\n    # create template maps for all days to find nearest x-y bins for dist_maps after predictions are made (cleaning)\n    temp_maps = np.nansum(maps, axis=2).astype(bool)\n    # initialize distance maps to show distance between actual and decoded position in each bin\n    mse, imse, coherence_maps = np.zeros_like(temp_maps).astype(float), np.zeros_like(temp_maps).astype(float),\\\n        np.zeros_like(temp_maps).astype(float)\n    for d in tqdm(range(n_days), desc=f'Performing {n_fold}-fold cross-validated position decoding'):\n        vel_idx[d, 1:] = gaussian_filter1d(np.linalg.norm((behav[1:, :, d] - behav[:-1, :, d]) * fps, axis=1),\n                                           axis=0, sigma=v_filt_size) > (v_thresh / bin_down)\n        cell_idx[d] = np.sum(traces[:, :, d][vel_idx[d]], axis=0) > cell_threshold\n        target_behav = behav[:, :, d][vel_idx[d]]\n        target_traces = traces[:, cell_idx[d], d][vel_idx[d]]\n        kf = KFold(n_splits=n_fold)\n        kf.get_n_splits(target_traces)\n        for fold, (train_index, test_index) in enumerate(kf.split(target_traces)):\n            # initialize maps to plot distance errors onto behavioural map\n            count_map, err_squared_map, prob_map = np.zeros_like(temp_maps[:, :, d]).astype(int), \\\n                                                   np.zeros_like(temp_maps[:, :, d]).astype(int), \\\n                                                   np.zeros_like(temp_maps[:, :, d]).astype(int)\n            # index target and train trace and behavioural data\n            behav_train, behav_test = target_behav[train_index], target_behav[test_index]\n            traces_train, traces_test = target_traces[train_index], target_traces[test_index]","stderr":"\nShell cwd was reset to /logs/verifier/judge/claude","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The AI loads the seven extension-less **joblib** files in `/app/data` (`QLAK-CA1-08`, `-30`, `-50`, `-51`, `-56`, `-74`, `-75`) rather than the parallel `.mat` files. These are the repository's own converted copies (`utils.mat2joblib` / `utils.load_dat(..., format=\"joblib\")`, which is the repo default). Each file is a dict keyed by the animal ID containing `SFPs`, `blocked`, `centroids`, `envs`, `maps`, `position`, `trace`. The subject list is hard-coded, and for each subject the whole dict is loaded into memory at once, then iterated day-by-day. Every animal, every recording day, and every complete 60-s window is kept; nothing is subsampled. After the loop the AI asserts the paper's totals (207 sessions, 69,744 session-neurons) and aborts if they are not reproduced.\n\nii.\n```python\nDATA_DIR = Path('/app/data')\nSUBJECTS = ['QLAK-CA1-08', 'QLAK-CA1-30', 'QLAK-CA1-50',\n            'QLAK-CA1-51', 'QLAK-CA1-56', 'QLAK-CA1-74',\n            'QLAK-CA1-75']\n...\nfor subj_i, subject in enumerate(SUBJECTS):\n    source = joblib.load(DATA_DIR / subject)[subject]\n    traces = source['trace']       # day x union-neuron x frame\n    positions = source['position'] # day x (x,y) x frame\n    envs = source['envs']\n    blocked = source['blocked']\n    for day in range(traces.shape[0]):\n        ...\n    del source, traces, positions\n    gc.collect()\n...\nif len(neural) != 207 or total_valid != 69744:\n    raise RuntimeError(f'unexpected source totals: {len(neural)} sessions, '\n                       f'{total_valid} session-neurons')\n```\n\niii. From the trajectory: \"The extensionless files are compressed serialization rather than plain pickle, likely joblib files used by the repository\" (step 5) and \"create `convert_data.py` using the smaller joblib-converted source files\" (step 8). The script docstring states: \"The repository's joblib files are used rather than reprocessing calcium video. They contain the paper's aligned 30-Hz binary rising-transient traces and DLC position, produced with the processing described in the paper.\" The AI first verified that all seven animals together give \"exactly the paper's 207 sessions and 5,413 tracked neurons\" (step 6) and later that the valid session-neuron count \"exactly reproduces the paper's 69,744 session-specific rate maps\" (step 11), and hard-coded both numbers as a guard.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects (mice)?\n\ni. One subject per data file / per animal ID. The seven IDs are hard-coded in `SUBJECTS` (verified beforehand by listing `/app/data`), written verbatim into `data['subjects']`, and each session appends the enclosing subject's index to `subject_idx`.\n\nii.\n```python\nSUBJECTS = ['QLAK-CA1-08', 'QLAK-CA1-30', 'QLAK-CA1-50',\n            'QLAK-CA1-51', 'QLAK-CA1-56', 'QLAK-CA1-74',\n            'QLAK-CA1-75']\n...\n        subject_idx.append(subj_i)\n...\n    'subjects': SUBJECTS,\n    'subject_idx': np.asarray(subject_idx, dtype=np.int64),\n```\n\niii. Step 3: \"The dataset contains seven animals (QLAK-CA1-08, 30, 50, 51, 56, 74, 75), each with a large MAT file and an extensionless processed file.\" The README states each file is named for an animal ID, and cells are tracked across days *within* an animal, so the file boundary is the subject boundary. The AI cross-checked the per-animal day counts against the paper's 207 sessions.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. One session per recording day, i.e. per index along the first axis of `trace` / `position` / `envs` / `blocked` (31 days for six animals, 21 for QLAK-CA1-51 → 207 sessions). Each day becomes one entry of `neural`/`input`/`output`/`brain_region_idx`, plus a `session_info` record holding subject, day index, environment name, blocked indices and frame counts.\n\nii.\n```python\n    traces = source['trace']       # day x union-neuron x frame\n    for day in range(traces.shape[0]):\n        tr = np.asarray(traces[day])\n        pos = np.asarray(positions[day])\n        ...\n        env = scalar_env(envs[day])\n        session_info.append({\n            'subject': subject,\n            'source_session_index': day,\n            'environment': env,\n            'blocked_grid_indices': [int(b) for b in block_values if b >= 0],\n            'source_frames': int(n_frames),\n            'used_frames': int(used),\n            'n_complete_one_minute_trials': int(n_trials),\n            'n_neurons_present': int(n_neurons),\n        })\n```\n\niii. The script docstring: \"A recording day is a session.\" The paper states \"All sessions were 40 min, and one session was recorded per day\", and each day is a different environment geometry, so day = session. Step 6: \"each recording day should remain a session.\" The AI confirmed the total equals the paper's 207 sessions.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Each session's continuous ~40-min recording is cut into **non-overlapping complete 60-s windows** (1800 frames at 30 Hz, stored as 60 one-second bins). The trailing partial minute is dropped (sessions have 71,866–72,219 frames → 39 or 40 complete trials; 8,187 trials in total). Splitting is done by a single `reshape` of the truncated arrays.\n\nii.\n```python\nFS = 30\nBIN_FRAMES = 30\nTRIAL_SECONDS = 60\nBINS_PER_TRIAL = TRIAL_SECONDS\nTRIAL_FRAMES = FS * TRIAL_SECONDS      # 1800\n...\n            n_trials = n_frames // TRIAL_FRAMES\n            if n_trials < 2:\n                raise ValueError(f'too few complete trials: {subject} day {day}')\n            used = n_trials * TRIAL_FRAMES\n            counts = tr[:, :used].reshape(\n                n_neurons, n_trials, BINS_PER_TRIAL, BIN_FRAMES\n            ).sum(axis=3, dtype=np.float32).transpose(1, 0, 2)\n```\n\niii. The instructions define the trial: \"long recording sessions, which will be split into 1-minute trials within each session.\" The docstring: \"Complete, non-overlapping 60-s windows are trials. A short trailing fragment is omitted so all trials have identical duration.\" Step 8: \"Sessions differ slightly in frame count, so only complete 1,800-frame (60 s at 30 Hz) trials should be retained; this yields 39 or 40 trials depending on actual recorded duration.\"\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No trial-level quality control is applied. The only exclusion is structural — an incomplete trailing window is dropped — and a defensive check aborts a session with fewer than two complete trials (never triggered; every session has 39–40). No velocity/immobility filter is applied, even though the paper's own Bayesian decoder (`utils.decode_position_within`, `v_thresh=5` cm/s) discards low-speed samples.\n\nii.\n```python\n            n_trials = n_frames // TRIAL_FRAMES\n            if n_trials < 2:\n                raise ValueError(f'too few complete trials: {subject} day {day}')\n            used = n_trials * TRIAL_FRAMES\n```\n\niii. Trials are an artificial construct imposed by the downstream decoder, not an experimental unit, so there is no behavioural criterion by which one minute of free foraging is \"bad\". The format requirement \"There needs to be at least two trials within each session\" is enforced explicitly. A speed filter would break the contiguity and fixed length of the 60-s windows, so it is not used.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Solely the `trace` field: the paper's rise-extracted binary calcium event traces, stored per animal as a `day × union-neuron × frame` array (values 0/1, NaN where a tracked cell was not registered that day). `SFPs`, `centroids` and `maps` are loaded with the file but not used.\n\nii.\n```python\n    traces = source['trace']       # day x union-neuron x frame\n    ...\n        tr = np.asarray(traces[day])\n```\n\niii. README: \"**trace**: rise-extracted calcium traces, where '1' indicates a significant event.\" Methods: \"This binary vector was treated as the firing rate in all further analyses.\" Step 4: \"Neural activity is already preprocessed into binary rising-phase calcium-event vectors\"; step 6 confirmed empirically that \"traces are binary\".\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. No re-derivation from fluorescence — the paper's binary event vector is taken as given. Processing is: (a) orientation check so the array is `(neuron, frame)`; (b) removal of absent (all-NaN) neurons (see 2-c); (c) **summation of the binary events within each non-overlapping 30-frame (1 s) window**, producing an integer-valued event count per neuron per second stored as `float32`; (d) transposition to per-trial `(n_neurons, 60)` matrices. Neural values therefore range 0–30 rather than {0,1}.\n\nii.\n```python\n            if tr.shape[1] != pos.shape[1] and tr.shape[0] == pos.shape[1]:\n                tr = tr.T\n            if tr.shape[1] != pos.shape[1]:\n                raise ValueError(f'unaligned streams: {subject} day {day}')\n            ...\n            counts = tr[:, :used].reshape(\n                n_neurons, n_trials, BINS_PER_TRIAL, BIN_FRAMES\n            ).sum(axis=3, dtype=np.float32).transpose(1, 0, 2)\n            ...\n            neural.append([counts[t] for t in range(n_trials)])\n```\nwith metadata `'neural_measurement': 'count per 1-s bin of paper-preprocessed binary rising-phase calcium transient events'`.\n\niii. Docstring: \"Thirty synchronized frames are aggregated per 1-s decoder bin. Binary neural events are summed (event counts), while x/y is averaged. This preserves event mass and avoids an impractically large 30-Hz decoder dataset.\" Step 6: \"raw 30 Hz would produce a prohibitively large decoder dataset, so we need … a justified common bin size (likely 1 second with event counts and mean position).\" Summation (rather than averaging) is chosen because the paper treats the binary vector as a firing rate, so a per-second sum is an event rate in Hz.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Per session, only neurons whose trace is entirely finite are retained; rows that are entirely NaN (cells from the animal's cross-day union that were not registered that day) are dropped. The code additionally asserts that no neuron is *partially* NaN. No other neuron-level curation is applied — no minimum event count, no place-cell selection — so session neuron counts run 113–564 and total 69,744 session-neurons, matching the paper's 69,744 rate maps. All neurons are assigned to the single brain region `CA1`.\n\nii.\n```python\n            finite = np.isfinite(tr)\n            valid = finite.all(axis=1)\n            if np.any(finite.any(axis=1) != valid):\n                raise ValueError(f'partially missing neuron: {subject} day {day}')\n            tr = tr[valid]\n            n_neurons, n_frames = tr.shape\n            ...\n            brain_region_idx.append(np.zeros(n_neurons, dtype=np.int64))\n            total_valid += n_neurons\n...\nif len(neural) != 207 or total_valid != 69744:\n    raise RuntimeError(...)\n```\n\niii. Docstring: \"Neurons tracked across animals' days occupy a union array; an all-NaN row means that cell was not observed that day. Such rows are removed session-wise. This gives the reported 69,744 rate maps.\" Step 8: \"Those columns must be removed separately per session; retaining them would introduce invalid values and would incorrectly treat unrecorded neurons as silent.\" The AI ran an audit over all seven animals before writing the converter (steps 9–11) and confirmed both the all-or-nothing NaN pattern and the 69,744 total. The paper applies no further neuron filter to its main analyses (its `cell_threshold=5` applies only inside its Bayesian decoder), so no activity threshold was imposed.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. There is no experimental alignment event — the recording is continuous free foraging. The AI defines the alignment event as the start of each 60-s window and records it explicitly, with `off_start = 0.0 s` and `off_end = 60.0 s`. Neural, input and output streams are cut with the same frame indices, so all trials cover exactly the same 60 s of wall-clock recording.\n\nii.\n```python\n    'metadata': {\n        ...\n        'temporal_alignment_event': 'start of each non-overlapping one-minute window',\n        'off_start': 0.0,\n        'off_end': 60.0,\n        'trial_definition': ('complete non-overlapping 60-s windows; trailing '\n                             'partial minute omitted'),\n```\n\niii. The instructions require `temporal_alignment_event`, `off_start` and `off_end`; with no stimulus or trial-onset event in this paradigm, the only well-defined reference point is the window boundary, and the AI states it rather than leaving the fields unset. Step 7/8 note that all streams are already timestamp-aligned at 30 Hz by the acquisition DAQ (\"The DAQ simultaneously acquired behavioral and cellular imaging streams at 30 Hz … all recorded frames were timestamped for post-hoc alignment\"), so a common frame index is sufficient for alignment.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. **Yes — the data are rebinned from the native 30 Hz to 1 Hz.** Thirty consecutive frames are pooled into one bin: neural events are summed, x/y position is averaged. Each trial is therefore `(n_neurons, 60)` instead of `(n_neurons, 1800)`, and `metadata['time_bin_size'] = 1000.0` ms (vs. the native 33.33 ms). The bin size is identical for every trial and session. This makes the saved dataset ~667 MB rather than ~20 GB.\n\nii.\n```python\nFS = 30\nBIN_FRAMES = 30\nBINS_PER_TRIAL = TRIAL_SECONDS          # 60 bins per trial\n...\n            counts = tr[:, :used].reshape(\n                n_neurons, n_trials, BINS_PER_TRIAL, BIN_FRAMES\n            ).sum(axis=3, dtype=np.float32).transpose(1, 0, 2)\n            mean_pos = pos[:, :used].reshape(\n                2, n_trials, BINS_PER_TRIAL, BIN_FRAMES\n            ).mean(axis=3)\n...\n        'time_bin_size': 1000.0,\n        'source_sampling_rate_hz': 30.0,\n```\n\niii. Docstring: \"Thirty synchronized frames are aggregated per 1-s decoder bin … This preserves event mass and avoids an impractically large 30-Hz decoder dataset.\" Step 5: \"retaining 30 Hz across all sessions would create a very large pickle and decoder workload, while the paper's maps use spatial rather than temporal binning.\" Step 11: \"use synchronized 1-second bins to keep decoder size tractable.\" The choice is motivated purely by dataset size / decoder tractability; the AI notes the paper itself temporally bins for decoding, though the paper's own decoder uses a 3-frame (100 ms) bin, 10× finer than the AI's.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Environment geometry* derived from?\n\ni. The `blocked` field — a per-day list of indices of the occluded cells of the 3×3 partition grid, with the sentinel `[-1]` for the unpartitioned square. `envs` (the geometry's string name, e.g. `square`, `o`, `t`, `l`, `glenn`) is read only for the `session_info` metadata, not for building the input.\n\nii.\n```python\n    blocked = source['blocked']\n    envs = source['envs']\n    ...\n            block_values = np.asarray(blocked[day], dtype=float).reshape(-1)\n            ...\n            env = scalar_env(envs[day])\n```\n\niii. README: \"**blocked**: location of blocked (occluded) partitions in 3x3 design of environment … organized in the following way – [[0, 1, 2], [3, 4, 5], [6, 7, 8]]. If no partitions are blocked, value is -1.\" Step 6: \"Environment geometry is explicitly supplied as blocked indices in a flattened 3x3 grid; square uses [-1] meaning no blocked bins\" — i.e. the geometry is available directly and need not be reconstructed from the environment name via `utils.get_env_mat`.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment geometry*?\n\ni. The blocked indices are converted to a fixed 9-element binary indicator vector in the same row-major `[[0,1,2],[3,4,5],[6,7,8]]` order as the source, with 1 = blocked. The `-1` sentinel (and any out-of-range value) is skipped, giving an all-zero vector for the open square. The vector is static per session and is replicated (a copy per trial) as each trial's `input`, shape `(9,)`; `input_names` are `blocked_spatial_bin_0..8`. Bin 7 is never blocked in this dataset, so that input is constant 0.\n\nii.\n```python\n            geometry = np.zeros(9, dtype=np.float32)\n            block_values = np.asarray(blocked[day], dtype=float).reshape(-1)\n            for b in block_values:\n                if np.isfinite(b) and 0 <= int(b) < 9:\n                    geometry[int(b)] = 1.0\n            ...\n            inputs.append([geometry.copy() for _ in range(n_trials)])\n...\n    'input_names': [f'blocked_spatial_bin_{i}' for i in range(9)],\n    'geometry_encoding': ('nine binary blocked-cell indicators in the same '\n                          'row-major index convention as source blocked lists'),\n```\n\niii. Docstring: \"Geometry is the supplied `blocked` list, encoded as nine binary indicators. The square's sentinel -1 means no blocked cells.\" The instructions ask for \"Environment geometry, representing which parts of the arena are blocked. Static per-trial\", so a nine-way binary code is the direct, lossless representation of all ten geometries; keeping the source's own index convention guarantees that input index *k* refers to the same physical cell as output class *k*.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Mouse position* derived from?\n\ni. The `position` field — DeepLabCut head-tracking x/y in centimetres in the 75 × 75 cm arena, stored `day × (x, y) × frame` at 30 Hz, one sample per imaging frame.\n\nii.\n```python\n    positions = source['position'] # day x (x,y) x frame\n    ...\n        pos = np.asarray(positions[day])\n        if pos.shape[0] != 2 and pos.shape[1] == 2:\n            pos = pos.T\n```\n\niii. README: \"**position**: x-y position data for all days … x-y position in first dimension, and number of temporal bins / frames in second dimension.\" Methods: \"Position data were generated from tracking the head with DeepLabCut.\" Step 8: \"Position is already aligned, finite, and scaled to 0–75 cm\" — the AI checked the range and NaN prevalence before using it, so no rescaling or interpolation is applied.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Mouse position*?\n\ni. Orientation is normalised to `(2, n_frames)` and the stream is checked to have the same frame count as the trace (hard error otherwise). Positions are then averaged within each 30-frame (1 s) bin and the *mean* position is discretised (see 4-c). No smoothing, no speed filtering, no unit conversion, and no interpolation of missing samples (there are none). The result is one integer class per second, stored per trial as shape `(1, 60)`.\n\nii.\n```python\n            mean_pos = pos[:, :used].reshape(\n                2, n_trials, BINS_PER_TRIAL, BIN_FRAMES\n            ).mean(axis=3)\n            xbin = np.clip(np.floor(mean_pos[0] / 25.0), 0, 2).astype(np.int64)\n            ybin = np.clip(np.floor(mean_pos[1] / 25.0), 0, 2).astype(np.int64)\n            spatial_class = ybin * 3 + xbin\n            ...\n            outputs.append([spatial_class[t][None, :] for t in range(n_trials)])\n...\n        'position_processing': ('mean aligned DLC x/y per 1-s bin, discretized '\n                                'at 25 and 50 cm into class y_bin*3+x_bin'),\n```\n\niii. Docstring and metadata: position is averaged over exactly the same 30 frames used to sum the neural events, so the behavioural and neural streams remain sample-for-sample synchronous. This mirrors the paper's own decoding pipeline, which average-pools position over frames (`AvgPool1d`) and then floor-divides into spatial bins — the AI uses a 30-frame pool where the paper uses 3.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Mouse position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The 75 cm arena is cut at 25 cm and 50 cm on each axis, giving a 3 × 3 grid of 25 cm cells. `x_bin = clip(floor(x/25), 0, 2)`, `y_bin = clip(floor(y/25), 0, 2)` (the clip catches the exact boundary value x = 75), and the class is `y_bin * 3 + x_bin`, i.e. row-major with y as row — the same convention as the `blocked` indices. There is one categorical output, `spatial_bin`, with 9 values named `row_r_column_c`. Class frequencies in the converted file range from 0.057 to 0.200.\n\nii.\n```python\n            xbin = np.clip(np.floor(mean_pos[0] / 25.0), 0, 2).astype(np.int64)\n            ybin = np.clip(np.floor(mean_pos[1] / 25.0), 0, 2).astype(np.int64)\n            spatial_class = ybin * 3 + xbin\n...\n    'output_names': ['spatial_bin'],\n    'output_values': [[f'row_{r}_column_{c}' for r in range(3) for c in range(3)]],\n```\n\niii. Docstring: \"The 75-cm square is divided at 25 and 50 cm, exactly matching the experiment's 3x3 construction. Class is row*3+column, with x selecting column and y row.\" The instructions require \"Mouse position discretized into 3 x 3 = 9 spatial bins\", and the paper built the arena as a 3 × 3 partition grid of 25 cm cells, so the decoder's bins coincide with the physical partition cells and with the geometry input's index convention. (Checking the data confirms the convention: occupancy is exactly zero in precisely the `blocked` indices under `y_bin*3+x_bin`.)\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Mouse position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Position and calcium frames are acquired and stored frame-synchronously, so alignment is by shared frame index: the same `used = n_trials * 1800` frames are taken from both streams, reshaped with the same `(n_trials, 60, 30)` layout, and the neural sum and position mean for bin *t* cover exactly the same 30 frames. A shape check aborts the conversion if the two streams ever disagree in frame count. The resulting per-trial arrays are `(n_neurons, 60)` and `(1, 60)`.\n\nii.\n```python\n            if tr.shape[1] != pos.shape[1] and tr.shape[0] == pos.shape[1]:\n                tr = tr.T\n            if tr.shape[1] != pos.shape[1]:\n                raise ValueError(f'unaligned streams: {subject} day {day}')\n            ...\n            counts = tr[:, :used].reshape(n_neurons, n_trials, BINS_PER_TRIAL, BIN_FRAMES).sum(axis=3, ...)\n            mean_pos = pos[:, :used].reshape(2, n_trials, BINS_PER_TRIAL, BIN_FRAMES).mean(axis=3)\n```\n\niii. Methods: \"The DAQ simultaneously acquired behavioral and cellular imaging streams at 30 Hz … and all recorded frames were timestamped for post-hoc alignment\", and step 8 confirmed the two arrays have identical frame counts per session, so no resampling or lag correction is needed; the docstring notes the 30 frames are \"synchronized\".\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Three cases are handled. (1) *Unregistered cells*: all-NaN neuron rows are dropped per session; partially-NaN rows would be a genuine anomaly and raise an error (verified never to occur across all 207 sessions). (2) *Ragged recording length*: sessions have 71,866–72,219 frames, so the trailing <60 s fragment is discarded (1,666–2,019 frames per session) to keep all trials exactly equal in length. (3) *Structural anomalies*: transposed arrays are silently corrected, but mismatched neural/position lengths, sessions with <2 complete trials, and any departure from the paper's 207/69,744 totals raise and abort the run rather than being papered over. Position has no missing samples (checked) and boundary values at exactly 75 cm are absorbed by `np.clip`; out-of-range `blocked` codes (the `-1` sentinel) are skipped.\n\nii.\n```python\n            finite = np.isfinite(tr)\n            valid = finite.all(axis=1)\n            if np.any(finite.any(axis=1) != valid):\n                raise ValueError(f'partially missing neuron: {subject} day {day}')\n            tr = tr[valid]\n            ...\n            n_trials = n_frames // TRIAL_FRAMES          # trailing fragment dropped\n            if n_trials < 2:\n                raise ValueError(f'too few complete trials: {subject} day {day}')\n            ...\n            xbin = np.clip(np.floor(mean_pos[0] / 25.0), 0, 2)\n            for b in block_values:\n                if np.isfinite(b) and 0 <= int(b) < 9:\n                    geometry[int(b)] = 1.0\n...\nif len(neural) != 207 or total_valid != 69744:\n    raise RuntimeError(...)\n```\n\niii. Step 8: NaN rows \"must be removed separately per session; retaining them would introduce invalid values and would incorrectly treat unrecorded neurons as silent\", and \"only complete 1,800-frame … trials should be retained\". The AI ran an explicit audit (steps 9–11) to establish that NaNs are strictly all-or-nothing per cell before relying on it, and encoded every assumption it had verified as a hard assertion so that a violation would fail loudly rather than corrupt the dataset. `session_info` records `source_frames` vs `used_frames` so the discarded remainder is auditable.\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The arithmetic (one `reshape`+`sum`, one `reshape`+`mean`, a few `clip`/`floor` calls per session) is negligible; the run is dominated by I/O and serialisation: (1) `joblib.load` of each animal file, which decompresses the *entire* dict — including `SFPs`, `centroids` and `maps` — and materialises the full `day × union-neuron × frame` float64 trace array (9.2–17.0 GB per animal, ~93 GB across the seven if all were held at once); (2) `pickle.dump` of the 667 MB output. Per-session work is `O(n_neurons × n_frames)` and single-pass. Memory is managed only coarsely, by `del` + `gc.collect()` between animals.\n\nii.\n```python\n        source = joblib.load(DATA_DIR / subject)[subject]   # decompresses whole animal dict\n        ...\n        del source, traces, positions\n        gc.collect()\n...\n    with OUT.open('wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f, protocol=pickle.HIGHEST_PROTOCOL)\n```\n\niii. Step 7 originally planned \"a streaming HDF5 converter to avoid loading multi-GB MAT files\", but step 8 switched to \"the smaller joblib-converted source files\" on the grounds that they are smaller on disk (99–151 MB compressed vs 296–794 MB for `.mat`). The docstring keeps only the scientific justification (\"They contain the paper's aligned 30-Hz binary rising-transient traces and DLC position\"); the peak-memory consequence of whole-file loading is not discussed. The AI did note (steps 12–13) that loading and serialising were the phases it had to wait on.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The numerically heavy work is already fully vectorised: binning and trial-splitting are one `reshape`/`sum` and one `reshape`/`mean` per session; discretisation is vectorised over the whole session at once. The remaining Python loops are shallow and unavoidable or trivial: the 7-subject × ~30-day outer loops (each iteration is an independent session), the ≤4-element loop over `block_values`, and three per-trial list comprehensions (`counts[t]`, `geometry.copy()`, `spatial_class[t][None, :]`) that only slice an already-computed array because the target format demands a Python list of per-trial arrays. None of these is worth vectorising further.\n\nii.\n```python\n            for b in block_values:\n                if np.isfinite(b) and 0 <= int(b) < 9:\n                    geometry[int(b)] = 1.0\n            neural.append([counts[t] for t in range(n_trials)])\n            inputs.append([geometry.copy() for _ in range(n_trials)])\n            outputs.append([spatial_class[t][None, :] for t in range(n_trials)])\n```\n\niii. Not discussed explicitly in the trajectory; the vectorised formulation follows from the AI's decision to express trial-splitting and binning as a single 4-D reshape (\"(neuron, trial, second, frame-within-second), then trial first\", per the inline comment), which performs splitting, binning and reordering in one pass instead of a per-trial loop.\n\n## 6-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. Essentially nothing is recomputed. Each session's trace and position are read once and reduced in a single pass; `n_neurons`/`n_frames`/`n_trials` are computed once and reused. The only duplication is representational: `geometry.copy()` creates `n_trials` identical 9-float vectors per session (≈8,187 copies, ~300 KB total) where one shared array would do, and `np.asarray(traces[day])` / `np.asarray(positions[day])` wrap already-materialised slices. `envs[day]` is parsed per session but only for metadata.\n\nii.\n```python\n            inputs.append([geometry.copy() for _ in range(n_trials)])\n            ...\n        tr = np.asarray(traces[day])\n        pos = np.asarray(positions[day])\n```\n\niii. Not discussed in the trajectory. The per-trial copy follows from the format requirement that `input` be a list with one entry per trial; copying rather than aliasing avoids any risk that a downstream consumer mutating one trial's input would silently alter every other trial in the session.\n\n## 6-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. Three items. (1) `joblib.load` deserialises the whole per-animal dict, so `SFPs` (35 × 35 × n_cells × n_days), `centroids` and the precomputed `maps` are decompressed and held in memory although the code only ever uses `trace`, `position`, `envs` and `blocked` — an h5py/`.mat` path would read only the needed datasets, session by session. (2) The trace is loaded for *all* union neurons including the all-NaN rows that are then discarded (roughly 65% of rows on a typical day for QLAK-CA1-08), and the full trace is truncated only after loading. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment geometry*?\n\ni. The blocked indices are converted to a fixed 9-element binary indicator vector in the same row-major `[[0,1,2],[3,4,5],[6,7,8]]` order as the source, with 1 = blocked. The `-1` sentinel (and any out-of-range value) is skipped, giving an all-zero vector for the open square. The vector is static per session and is replicated (a copy per trial) as each trial's `input`, shape `(9,)`; `input_names` are `blocked_spatial_bin_0..8`. Bin 7 is never blocked in this dataset, so that input is constant 0.\n\nii.\n```python\n            geometry = np.zeros(9, dtype=np.float32)\n            block_values = np.asarray(blocked[day], dtype=float).reshape(-1)\n            for b in block_values:\n                if np.isfinite(b) and 0 <= int(b) < 9:\n                    geometry[int(b)] = 1.0\n            ...\n            inputs.append([geometry.copy() for _ in range(n_trials)])\n...\n    'input_names': [f'blocked_spatial_bin_{i}' for i in range(9)],\n    'geometry_encoding': ('nine binary blocked-cell indicators in the same '\n                          'row-major index convention as source blocked lists'),\n```\n\niii.
